# PassAuth Architecture

PassAuth is a self-hosted, zero-knowledge password manager. The server stores only ciphertext and can never read vault contents, item names, or URLs.

## Components

| Component | Tech | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `crates/core` | Rust | All crypto, item model, importers. One implementation shared by every client. |
| `crates/server` | Rust (axum, tokio, sqlx) | REST API + WebSocket sync, SMTP, admin. Serves the web vault. |
| `crates/core-wasm` | Rust → WebAssembly | `core` bindings for the web vault and extension. |
| `web/` | TypeScript, React, Vite | Web vault, embedded into the server binary. |
| `extension/` | TypeScript, MV3 (Chrome, Firefox) | Login, autofill, TOTP, passkey provider. |
| `crates/core-ffi` | Rust → UniFFI | `core` bindings (Kotlin and Swift) for the native apps. |
| `android/` (stage 2) | Kotlin, Jetpack Compose | Android app; see "Android app" below. |
| `ios/` (stage 2) | Swift, SwiftUI | iOS app; see "iOS app" below. |
| `desktop/` | Rust (Tauri 2) + the web vault | macOS, Windows and Linux app; see "Desktop app" below. |

### Why Rust
- Memory-safe without a garbage collector: fast, small container, low RAM.
- The same crypto code compiles to WASM (browser, extension) and native libraries (iOS, Android), so there is exactly one audited implementation of the vault format.
- Mature, audited crates: RustCrypto (`argon2`, `chacha20poly1305`, `hkdf`, `x25519-dalek`, `ed25519-dalek`), `opaque-ke`, `webauthn-rs`, `lettre`.

## Cryptography

### Account keys
```
master password ─┐
                 ├─ Argon2id (m=128 MiB, t=3, p=1, salt=random 16 B per account)
secret key (optional per user, 128-bit, in Emergency Kit) ─ HKDF ─ XOR ─► stretched key
stretched key ─ HKDF("passauth/auk/v1") ─► Account Unlock Key (AUK)
stretched key ─ HKDF("passauth/opaque-password/v1") ─► OPAQUE password input
AUK ─► decrypts Account Key (random 256-bit)
Account Key ─► decrypts X25519 keypair (sharing)
Account Key ─► decrypts the Ed25519 signing key seed (signed sharing; wrapped_signing_key)
Vault Key (random 256-bit per vault) ─ wrapped by Account Key, or sealed to each member's X25519 key
Item Key (random 256-bit per item) ─ wrapped by Vault Key ─► encrypts item JSON
Send Key (random 256-bit per Send, only in the link's #fragment) ─ wrapped by Account Key (owner_key)
Send Content Key (v2, Sends with a password) = HKDF(Send Key, salt = HKDF(Argon2id(password))) ─ wrapped by Account Key (owner_content_key)
File Key (random 256-bit per attachment, inside the encrypted item) ─ encrypts the file (STREAM, 64 KiB chunks)
Emergency Key (random 256-bit per emergency contact) ─ wrapped by Account Key (grantor_key), sealed to the contact's X25519 key (sealed_key)
Vault Key of each personal vault ─ also wrapped by each Emergency Key (emergency_vault_keys)
```
- Secret Key is **off by default**. Users turn it on in settings. The admin can require it (`require_secret_key`, see Policies). Enabling it re-wraps the Account Key under a new AUK; no vault data is re-encrypted.
- Cipher: **XChaCha20-Poly1305** everywhere. No unauthenticated modes, no legacy fallbacks.
- AEAD associated data binds `{format_version, item_id, vault_id}`, so ciphertexts cannot be swapped between items or fields.
- The whole item (name, URLs, username, password, TOTP, passkeys, notes, fields) is one encrypted blob. Plaintext on the server is limited to IDs, vault, revision, timestamps and sizes (the item type, folder and favorite are inside the ciphertext).
- Unknown item fields are kept (`extra` on every item struct) and a `format` above the client's own is never stamped down, so an older client editing an item written by a newer one doesn't erase the newer fields. Unknown enum values (e.g. a new URL match type) still fail to decode; add those behind a format bump.
- **Item edit counter** (`rev`, security round item 9): every item's encrypted JSON carries `rev` (u64, missing = 0), so the AEAD authenticates it and the server can neither read nor change it. The core sets it on every encryption: `EncryptedItem::encrypt` (WASM `encryptItem`, UniFFI `encrypt_item`) writes `rev` + 1 of the item given (1 for a new item; a move to another vault and a key replacement re-encrypt the *decrypted* item, so + 1), `update` (`updateItem` / `update_item`) decrypts the stored item itself and writes one more than the higher of its `rev` and the given item's (so an app that dropped the field still counts up, and restoring an earlier version counts up from the current one), `move_to` + 1. Apps from before keep it as an unknown field through an edit (equal counter, other content: never flagged). The counter is read as at most 2^53 − 2 (`MAX_ITEM_REV`; a stored number beyond it, a fraction or a negative one is clamped, since anyone with the vault key can store any number) and a write stores at most one more, so no value of `rev` makes an item impossible to open, edit, move or re-encrypt, and every binding carries the number exactly; at the top the counter stays where it is. The server's own `revision` stays the concurrency check; it can't go into the AAD because the server assigns it after encryption. Detection is the apps' device memory (like the key pins' highest roster): per account, item id → highest `rev` seen, checked with `item_rev_check(remembered, served)` (WASM `itemRevCheck`, UniFFI `item_rev_check`) → `new` / `same` / `newer` (remember it) or `rollback` (lower: "Your server sent an older version of this item." — keep the newer offline copy if there is one, show the item with that warning plus a sync-level notice, never remember the lower number). Item history versions are older by nature and are not checked; a restore is an `updateItem` of the current item. A new device trusts what it sees first; in a vault without a manifest (next point) a server can still withhold an item's newest version from a device that never saw it, or hide deletions (tombstones aren't counted).
- **Vault manifests** (migration 17, feature `vault_manifest`; core `crates/core/src/manifest.rs`, server `crates/server/src/vault/manifest.rs`): a small sealed record of what a vault holds, so a server can't serve an older copy of an item, hide a new item or a deletion, or bring back a deleted or trashed item on a device that saw the newer state, and can't move a device from the history it saw onto another one. **Record:** `{version, live, trashed, acc}`: the counts of items outside and inside the trash, and `acc`, the sum mod 2^256 (big-endian) of one term per item, `HMAC-SHA256(mac key, item id ‖ 0x00 ‖ state ‖ 0x00 ‖ SHA-256(data))` with `state` 0 = live, 1 = in the trash, and `data` the item's `data` text exactly as stored (its UTF-8 bytes). The wrapped item key, `deleted_at`'s value and the server's `revision` are not covered. An item is in the trash exactly when its row's `deleted_at` is not null, in the core and in every app's own lists alike; the core refuses a row whose `deleted_at` is anything but null/absent or a whole number above zero (0, a negative number, a fraction, text), and reads no other field for it, so no row shape counts as live in one place and trashed in another. Additive, so a write changes it without the other items: subtract the old term, add the new one. Both keys come from the vault key (HKDF-SHA256, no salt; info `passauth/vault-manifest-mac/v1` and `passauth/vault-manifest-key/v1`). The server can compute neither a term nor the sum and never sees them. **Chain** (security review; closes "fork then join"): every manifest names the one it replaces. A manifest's *link* is `SHA-256("passauth/vault-manifest-link/v1" ‖ 0x00 ‖ version (8 bytes, big-endian) ‖ prev (32 bytes) ‖ SHA-256(sealed text))`, where `sealed text` is its sealed form as stored (the base64url characters) and `prev` the link of the manifest it replaces (32 zero bytes for a vault's first). The record is sealed (XChaCha20-Poly1305, base64url) as JSON with the associated data `passauth/vault-manifest/v1/<vault id>/<key epoch>/<version>/<prev as 64 hex digits>`, so opening the newest manifest (the head) authenticates the link its writer built on; the server stores `prev` in clear (`vaults.manifest_prev`, sent as `manifest_prev`) because the app needs it to open the manifest. An ordinary write names the head it was computed from; a key replacement names the old key's head; a baseline names whatever head the server showed (its link needs no opening). Every manifest, a first one, a key replacement's and a baseline included, is exactly one version above the one it replaces. The server keeps a log, `vault_manifest_log (vault_id, seq, prev, digest, created_at)` with `digest` = SHA-256 of the sealed text, one entry per stored manifest. It keeps every entry younger than 90 days and at least the newest 5000 of a vault, up to 200,000 entries a vault (the oldest go first beyond that; pruned inside a write's transaction at every 64th version, deleted with the vault), so a burst of writes can't cut off a device that was away for a short while, and serves it as `GET /vaults/{id}/manifest-log?after=<seq>[&limit=<n>]` (any member; 404 otherwise) → `{entries: [{seq, prev, digest}], more}`, oldest first, at most 2000 an answer. Every earlier link follows from an entry by hashing, so the chain is followed without opening earlier manifests, which may be sealed under vault keys the device never had. The server itself refuses a manifest whose `prev` isn't the link of the vault's current one (409 `manifest_conflict`), on every path, so an honest server never stores a manifest off the chain; the apps don't rely on that. **Server:** stores the sealed text, its version and `prev` in `vaults.manifest` / `manifest_seq` (0 = none) / `manifest_prev` and returns them on every vault row of `/sync` (full and delta, read in the same snapshot as the items; also in the emergency-access view). Once a vault has a manifest, every request that changes its items carries the vault's next one, exactly one version up and naming the current one, stored in the same transaction under the vault's row lock (`VaultWrite::manifests`): `POST /items`, `POST /items/import`, `PUT /items/{id}` (a move: one for each of the two vaults that has a manifest), `POST /vaults/{id}/rotate` and `.../members/{user}/remove` (computed afresh with the new key and sealed for the new epoch, because the keys change with the vault key), `POST /vaults/{id}/trash/purge` in the body as `manifests: [{vault_id, seq, prev, sealed}]`, and `DELETE /items/{id}`, `POST /items/{id}/restore`, `DELETE /items/{id}/permanent` (no body) in the query as `manifest_seq` + `manifest_prev` + `manifest`. Without it: 400 `manifest_required` (message "Update PassAuth to change this vault.", which apps from before show; 400 rather than 409 so they don't offer to overwrite); not the next version, not naming the current manifest, or a manifest for a vault that has none: 409 `manifest_conflict` (sync, check, compute again, retry; at most 3 times). An item's own `revision_conflict` is reported first. A retried create or import that finds its items stores nothing and needs no manifest. Answers add `manifest_seq` and `manifest_link`, the version and the link of the vault's manifest after the request (0 and null without one; import: `manifest_seqs` and `manifest_links` per vault too; a move: the new vault's, plus `manifest_links` for both). An app applies its own write to its copy, and remembers the manifest it sent, only when `manifest_link` is the link of what it sent; otherwise it syncs. The version alone doesn't say that: a retried create whose first attempt was stored answers with the same version also when the retry carried another sealing of the manifest, and an app that then remembered its second sealing would be on a history the server never had (a false warning at the next sync). Renaming, sharing, attachments and item history don't touch the manifest (attachments and item history are separate tables and are **not** covered by it). **Starting one:** `PUT /vaults/{id}/manifest {base_seq, seq, prev, sealed, vault_seq}` (write access) stores a vault's first manifest (`base_seq` 0, `seq` 1, `prev` all zero) or a new baseline over the current one (`seq` = `base_seq` + 1, `prev` = its link); `vault_seq` is the vault's `seq` in the sync answer it was computed from, so any write in between is a 409 `manifest_conflict`. `seq` must be `base_seq` + 1 (400 otherwise): no request makes a version jump, so the only way a vault's version rises is one write at a time, and its ceiling (2^53 − 1) is out of any member's reach. Baselines are limited to 10 an hour for each member in each vault (429 with `Retry-After`; counted only when one is stored, and per member, so a member who uses up their own can't keep the others from repairing the vault). A first one is refused (409 `manifest_not_ready`) until every active session of every active member **who can write** comes from an app that keeps manifests (`auth.rs` `KEEPS_MANIFESTS`: web 0.15.0, desktop and macOS AutoFill 0.13.0, extension, iOS and Android 0.12.0; a session that never named its app counts as old; members who only read never send a manifest, so their apps don't hold it off), which `/sync` shows per vault as `manifest_ok` — a compatibility gate like `account_wraps_v2`, so no signed-in app loses the ability to write by surprise; a lying server can only delay the start. One old or unnamed session of any member with write access holds the start off for as long as it stays signed in, and neither the owner nor the admin is shown whose it is (the vault simply works without a manifest meanwhile); members update their apps or sign out old devices (Settings → Signed-in devices). An app with write access starts it only when `manifest_ok` is true, the vault passed the signed-sharing checks and it holds every item. Logged as `vault.manifest_started` / `vault.manifest_reset`. **Old trash:** the server never changes the items of a vault with a manifest by itself, so the trash sweeper skips such vaults; `/sync` carries `server_time` and `trash_days`, and apps with write access delete what is due after a sync that checked out, as one `POST /vaults/{id}/trash/purge {items: [{id, revision}], manifests}` (all or nothing; also how an app empties the trash). So in a vault with a manifest old trash stays until an app of a member with write access opens the vault. Because `trash_days` and `deleted_at` are the server's word, an app never deletes an item it has itself seen in the trash for less than max(`trash_days`, 7) days: a server that lies about either can't empty a trash sooner than a week after the device first saw the item there. **Checking** (every app, every sync, readers too; core `manifest::verify`, WASM `VaultKey.manifestVerify`, UniFFI `manifest_verify`): after applying a full or delta answer the app passes the vault row's `manifest_seq`, `manifest_prev` and `manifest`, what this device remembers for the vault (the version and link of the last manifest it checked or wrote itself), the vault's complete item set and, when it has fetched one, the log. In this order: a lower version than remembered, or no manifest after one was seen → `rollback`; no manifest and nothing remembered → `none`; a manifest that doesn't open for this vault, epoch, version and `prev` → `mismatch`; the remembered version with another link (a different manifest at the same version) → `mismatch`; the very next version naming another predecessor → `mismatch` (nothing can lie between the two, so no log is asked for); a newer version whose `prev`, followed back through the log by hashes (versions falling, never below the remembered one), doesn't arrive at the remembered link → `mismatch`; counts or sum differ → `mismatch`; an item without data or hash → `incomplete`; a version two or more ahead that doesn't name the remembered manifest directly and no log given → `need_log` (the app fetches the log after the remembered version and checks again; an answer isn't taken as checked meanwhile, and a log that can't be fetched leaves the vault as it was before this sync); otherwise `ok` (remember this version and link). A log entry that isn't `{seq, prev, digest}` of the right kinds is skipped, never an error (only an unbroken run of hashes counts, so rubbish can't help or hurt); a remembered link of 32 zero bytes is refused as no memory at all (no manifest has that link, and it would follow any history that begins above the remembered version). `trash=summary` rows come without `data`; the server adds `data_sha256` (base64url) for them and the term is computed from it — sound without trusting the server, because a wrong hash changes the sum; a cached row with neither (`incomplete`) makes the app fetch that vault in full once. Device memory: per account and vault `{version, link}`, kept with the item edit counters. On `rollback` or `mismatch` the vault stays readable with a vault-level warning ("Your server's copy of this vault doesn't match its record of changes. Items may be missing, restored or out of date.") and writes to it are held back. **Clearing a warning ("Use the server's copy")** is two different things, and which one applies follows from one check, the vault as the server serves it now judged with nothing remembered (core `manifest::accept`, `manifestAccept` / `manifest_accept`). (1) *The served vault stands on its own* (its manifest opens and records exactly the served items; or the server shows no manifest at all): the acceptance is the device's own business. After the user's confirmation the device replaces its memory for the vault with the served manifest's version and link (or forgets the vault when there is none), also when it remembered a higher version, and goes on from there. Nothing is written to the server, so every member can do it on every device and in every app, readers and the extension included. This is the case after a restored backup, for a device further behind than the log reaches, and for a device that was shown another history. (2) *The served manifest is wrong in itself* (it doesn't open, or the items aren't what it records): nothing can be accepted locally, and a member with write access writes a new baseline over the served items (`PUT …/manifest`, one version above the served manifest and naming it, whatever the device remembered), then remembers the baseline in place of its old memory. Devices on the served history follow the baseline by themselves (directly or through the log); every other device is then in case (1) and accepts on its own. A reader in case (2) waits for someone who can edit. So a warning can always be cleared: by the device alone when the server's copy is consistent, and by one write of any member with write access when it isn't; the only state without a remedy in the apps is a server that withholds what the checks need. Memory is replaced, not merged, on these two occasions only (apps that share the memory between processes mark the entry so that an older in-process copy with a higher version can't win it back); everywhere else a version never goes down. The chain stays sound through both: a device's memory is always a manifest it checked against the served items or sealed itself, everything later must descend from it, and what was known before is given up on the user's word alone. An item that doesn't open (a wrapped key that is rubbish; a member with write access can store one, and the manifest covers only `data`) doesn't change the manifest's verdict and doesn't hold writes (final review): an app that already holds the row with the same `data` keeps its own key and shows nothing; otherwise the item is listed as "Item that can't be opened" with a notice on the vault, a row that decrypts but can't be read is listed as "Item from a newer version of PassAuth" without a notice, and no first manifest is started over a row that doesn't decrypt. A member with write access can remove such an item like any other, by `DELETE /items/{id}/permanent` (or trash and `POST …/trash/purge`) with the next manifest, whose `removed` entry is the row by its id, `deleted_at` and `data`; nothing needs to be opened for that, and an app doesn't start a first manifest over such rows. **Writing:** `manifestNext` / `manifest_next` opens the row's manifest and returns `{seq, prev, sealed, link}` for `removed` (the items as they were) and `added` (as they are after the write); `manifestStart` / `manifest_start` seals one for a whole item set on a given `prev` (first manifest: none; baseline and key replacement: `manifestLink` / `manifest_link` of the vault row). The app sends `seq`, `prev`, `sealed` and, once the server stored it, remembers `seq` and `link`. **What the chain proves, and what it doesn't:** the head's `prev` is authenticated by the vault key; each log entry counts only where its hash is the `prev` of the entry (or head) above it, so the server can't splice in, leave out, reorder, renumber or invent entries without a SHA-256 collision, and a log cut short (pruned, or withheld) gives `mismatch`, never a pass. Each manifest on the chain was named by the app that wrote the next one, which had checked it against its items (a baseline excepted, by the user's choice). The link binds the version, so a log can't relabel entries, and the head's version is in the associated data. **Limits:** a new device (and the emergency-access view, which has no memory) trusts the first manifest it sees; the server can keep groups of devices on separate histories, or one consistent earlier state on a device that never saw a later one, and a device warns when it is moved onto a history that doesn't contain the last state it saw; a device further behind than the log reaches (entries older than 90 days beyond the newest 5000, or beyond 200,000) gets the warning even from an honest server (it can't tell a pruned log from a withheld one) and accepts the server's copy once; accepting the server's copy is trust in the server for that one state (an older consistent state of the vault passes it, by design: that is what a restored backup is); manifests are sealed, not signed, and roles are enforced by the server only: anyone holding the vault key, a read-only member included, who works with the server can seal a matching manifest for an altered item set (the sum is not collision-resistant against a key holder either), and a member with write access can store one that doesn't match on an honest server (everyone then sees the warning until a member with write access writes a baseline; a member who keeps doing it can keep the vault warned until removed, as such a member could also delete its items); `deleted_at`'s value, `server_time` and `trash_days` are the server's word, so a server can make apps delete trashed items early (only items already in the trash); attachments, item history, vault names and membership are outside it. Golden vectors: `vaultManifest` in `testdata/vectors.json` (links, chains, forks at the same version and joined later with and without the log, pruned logs and logs with rubbish in them, key replacement and baseline, `accepts`: the check behind "Use the server's copy", memories that may not be used, rows that may not be counted).
- Item JSON carries a `format` number (`ITEM_FORMAT` in `crates/core/src/item.rs`). Clients upgrade older items when they decrypt them and stamp the current format when they encrypt. The server can't read items, so schema changes never need a server migration. Format 2 (0.2.1): a website's URL match `default` follows the client's "Default URI match detection" setting; older items, saved when `domain` was the implicit default, read as `default`.
- **Send** (`crates/core/src/send.rs`): the send key is 32 random bytes in the link's fragment. `name` and `data` are each `base64url(0x01 ‖ nonce ‖ ct ‖ tag)` with associated data `passauth/send/v1/<field>` (the id is chosen by the server later, so it isn't bound; every Send has its own key). `owner_key` is the send key wrapped by the Account Key (`passauth/send-key/v1`). Password: `access_salt` = `sendpw-v1$m=…,t=…,p=…$<salt>`; `access_verifier` = HKDF-SHA256(Argon2id(password), salt, `passauth/send-password/v1`); visitors' apps accept `access_salt` only between 64–256 MiB, 3–6 passes and 1–4 lanes (`VISITOR_MAX_*`, else `kdf_too_strong`; the app writes 128 MiB, 3 passes, 1 lane), so a Send can't make a visitor's tab derive 1 GiB × 20. **Send v2** (migration 16, feature `send_v2`; closes security review L2 for v2 Sends): a Send with a password encrypts `name` and `data` with a content key that needs the password too. From the same Argon2id output as the verifier: `pw_key` = HKDF-SHA256(Argon2id(password), salt, `passauth/send-password-key/v2`) (never leaves the device), `content_key` = HKDF-SHA256(ikm = send key, salt = `pw_key`, info `passauth/send-content/v2`); each field is `base64url(0x02 ‖ 0x01 ‖ nonce ‖ ct ‖ tag)` with associated data `passauth/send/v2/<field>` (versions told by the first byte, no trial decryption). `owner_content_key` = the content key wrapped by the Account Key (`passauth/send-content-key/v1`), so the owner's apps list and edit the Send without the password; `owner_key` stays (the owner can still show and copy the link). Apps write v2 for every new Send with a password, and when setting or changing a password (new salt, new content key, text re-encrypted). On a server that doesn't list `send_v2` (an older server would drop `owner_content_key`) they refuse instead ("Update your PassAuth server to protect a Send with a password."): a Send with a password is never written as v1, and a v2 Send is never rewritten as v1 while it keeps a password, so hiding the feature can't downgrade one. Removing the password re-encrypts the text with the send key (v1) and clears `owner_content_key`. Sends without a password and v1 Sends with one (from before; the owner can't upgrade them without the password, so they stay v1 until the password is set again) work exactly as before. Visitors: `sendPasswordKeys(password, access_salt, link key)` → `{verifier, contentKey}` in one Argon2id run, send the verifier, then `sendOpen(link key, contentKey, field, ciphertext)` opens either format. What remains for v2: whoever has the link *and* the server's data (the stored verifier hash, the ciphertext) can guess the password offline, each guess one Argon2id run with the Send's parameters (64–256 MiB). A dishonest server can also make that cheaper for a password a visitor types (known, not fixed): `access_salt` is the server's word to the visitor, so it can hand out the weakest parameters the visitor's app accepts (64 MiB, 3 passes, 1 lane) and a salt of its choice, and take the verifier the visitor's app then sends as the target of its guessing (with the link, which it doesn't have by itself, also against the content key). Adding a password to an existing v1 Send can't protect text that holders of the link could already read: the earlier ciphertext was open to them, and to a server that kept it. For v1 Sends with a password L2 stays as it was (the link and the server's data read the text). Apps from before can't read v2 Sends' names and text (their owner's older apps show them as unreadable; they can still turn them off or delete them). The server can also roll back an edited Send to its earlier text (I4, accepted).
- **Attachments** (`crates/core/src/file.rs`): XChaCha20-Poly1305 in the STREAM construction. Header `0x01 ‖ 19-byte random prefix`; 64 KiB chunks with nonce `prefix ‖ u32 BE counter ‖ last flag` and associated data `passauth/file/v1/<item id>/<attachment id>` (the vault isn't bound, so moved items keep their files). Encrypted size = 20 + size + 16·chunks (`fileEncryptedSize`, the `size` sent to the server). A chunk's plaintext is released only after its tag checks; cut-short, extended or reordered files fail; discard all output unless `finish()` succeeds. Use a new key per upload. The format byte isn't in the associated data; any v2 format must bind its version. Streaming API `FileEncryptor`/`FileDecryptor` (`push`/`finish`) in WASM and UniFFI.
- **Emergency access** (`keys.rs`, v2): confirming a contact makes an `EmergencyKey` for that access (random 256 bits, zeroized, bound to its grantor and access) in the grantor's app, after the fingerprint check. It is wrapped by the Account Key (`grantor_key`, associated data `passauth/emergency-key/v2/<grantor id>/<access id>`) so any of the grantor's apps can open it again, sealed to the contact's X25519 key (`sealed_key`, `passauth/emergency-key-sealed/v2/<grantor id>/<access id>`), and wraps the key of each personal vault (`passauth/emergency-vault-key/v2/<grantor id>/<access id>/<vault id>`; a personal vault is one the grantor owns with their own key that nobody else is in, not even invited; with signed sharing also no trusted roster listing anyone else and no `signed_since` in its epoch pin, decided from the same member list the wraps and any key replacement then use: Signed sharing step 7). Ids must be non-empty and without `/`. API: `UnlockedAccount::new_emergency_key(access) → {key, wrapped}`, `open_emergency_key(access, wrapped)`, `open_sealed_emergency_key(grantor, access, sealed)`, `seal_emergency_roster(json)` / `open_emergency_roster(sealed)`; `EmergencyKey::seal_for(contact public key, grantor, access)` (only on the key `new_emergency_key` made: a key opened again by the grantor's apps or by the contact can't be sealed to a key pair of the caller's; refuses malformed and low-order keys and another grantor or access), `wrap_vault_key(vault key, vault)`, `open_vault_key(vault, wrapped)`, `grantor_id()`, `grant_id()`; WASM `Account.newEmergencyKey/openEmergencyKey/openSealedEmergencyKey/sealEmergencyRoster/openEmergencyRoster`, `EmergencyKey.sealFor/wrapVaultKey/openVaultKey/grantorId/grantId`, the same in UniFFI. The emergency key opens only those vault keys: never the Account Key, the private key, shared vaults or Sends. The **roster** is the grantor's list of accesses their apps may add wraps for, sealed with the Account Key (`passauth/emergency-roster/v2/<account id>`; JSON `{version, accesses: [{id, contact_key, grantor_key_hash, email, name}]}`, `grantor_key_hash` = base64url SHA-256 of the `grantor_key` string, `email`/`name` as the grantor saw them when confirming; entries from before have neither and show "Unknown contact" with the key's fingerprint), so the server keeps it but can neither read nor change it: a server (or a restored backup) that lists a removed access again with its old `grantor_key` gets no new wraps for it. Every roster write and every fill drops the accesses the server no longer lists in any state (ended by either side or with an account) and keeps them in the roster's `removed` list (`[{id, grantor_key_hash}]`, which also gets every contact the grantor removes and is never pruned), so one that ended can't be replayed later either: fills, "Confirm again" and confirming refuse a removed id or key hash, and the app says "<name> was removed from your emergency contacts. To give them access again, add them as a new contact." `removed` keeps the newest 300 (the oldest go first; when merging, the server's list comes before this device's own, so this device's memory goes last). A roster that would pass the 48 KiB limit is refused ("Your list of emergency contacts is full…"), never trimmed: names and emails come from the server, so each entry keeps them on one line without invisible characters, at most 254 (email) and 100 (name) characters, and padding them can't push removed contacts out. A server that hides a live access only denies it service (the contact is then added again as a new one). Accesses that hold keys but are neither in the roster nor removed (a write that got lost, or after "Start a new list") get "Confirm again" (the fingerprint check of confirming, then an entry for the access's existing `grantor_key`, which must open under the Account Key for that access; no new key). Pruning also keeps the roster small. Accepted gap: a contact who stepped down can be replayed until one of the grantor's devices next runs a fill (the grantor never removed them, so they're back where the grantor last left them). Removing a contact who held the key makes the grantor's app replace the keys of the personal vaults it opened (server `rotation_pending`), so a contact who kept it can't read what is added afterwards (what they read before stays read; files keep their own keys, which their items carry); sharing a vault that had wraps replaces its key at once, too. Threat model (security review M3/L5): the waiting period and approval are server policy, so a compromised or colluding server can hand the sealed key and the wraps out at once: that opens the personal vaults wrapped for the access, nothing else. Which vaults are personal comes from the server (the apps check it against their own sync and the vault's members list), so a compromised server could also get a shared vault wrapped by claiming nobody else is in it. Rollback: each device remembers the highest roster version it opened and its last trusted roster itself (sealed; per account, local storage) and adds no wraps from an older roster; a device with no memory trusts the first roster it opens, and a device that never saw the newer one can't tell, so restoring a server backup brings back the contacts listed in it for such devices, while devices that saw the removal refuse it. After a real restore, "Use the server's list" (on the vault notice and the Emergency page) shows that roster's contacts with the names, emails and key fingerprints from inside it (never the server's listing), marks those the device's last trusted roster doesn't have ("Would get access again"), and, with the master password (a reauth), reads and opens it again and writes exactly the entries it showed (less those removed and those the server no longer lists; entries it didn't show, because the server didn't list them at the review, go to `removed`, as do everything either list removed) forward as a new roster one version above the newest the device saw (compare-and-swap; on `roster_changed` the list is read and shown again, never written unseen), then remembers that version: the device fills and confirms again, and every older version, the ones in between included, stays refused. Only a roster that opens under the Account Key is taken; with no roster at all, the new one is empty. A roster that doesn't open (garbage from a stolen session or the server) offers "Start a new list" instead (master password; an empty roster above the newest version seen, keeping the `removed` list of the device's last trusted roster), after which contacts that hold keys and weren't removed show "Confirm again". The web vault's own code is served by the server: a compromised server can serve changed code, so these protections (and the promise that a removed contact can't read what is added afterwards) hold fully only in the desktop app, whose code ships with the app; the confirm and remove texts say "unless your server is later compromised". The box is anonymous, so a server can also forge one and show a fake grantor vault: apps show emergency data read-only and never autofill it. v1 (until migration 13) sealed the Account Key itself (`passauth/emergency-account-key/v1/<grantor id>`); those accesses carry `v1_exposed`, and the apps say for good that such a contact may keep the account key, which no key change takes back, instead of promising that removing them protects what is added afterwards. The confirm step says: "Confirming gives <name> a key to your personal vaults. Once they get access (or at once, if your server is ever compromised), they can read everything in those vaults. Shared vaults and Sends stay out of reach. If you remove them, PassAuth changes the keys of those vaults, so they can't read anything you add afterwards, unless your server is later compromised."
- Sealed boxes refuse low-order public keys (an all-zero shared secret would make the box readable by anyone); this also hardens vault sharing (`shareTo`).
- **KDF policy**: `createAccountWithKdf` / `rewrapWithKdf` use memory = max(128 MiB, `min_kdf_memory_mib`) and passes = max(3, `min_kdf_iterations`) and refuse minimums outside 64–1024 MiB or 3–20; fixing a `kdf` violation = `rewrapWithKdf` with the same password.
- KDF parameters are authenticated: clients enforce minimums and refuse to log in with weaker parameters than they already know.

### Authentication: OPAQUE (`opaque-ke`)
- The server never receives the password or a hash it could crack offline.
- The AUK and the OPAQUE password input are two HKDF outputs of the same Argon2id result (domain-separated), so the server's OPAQUE record never yields the AUK. The OPAQUE export key is not used. Consequence: a leak of the `users.bundle` column alone allows offline guessing at Argon2id cost when the Secret Key is off, which is why the Secret Key is recommended.
- Sessions: a random access token (15 min) and a **rotating** refresh token tied to the device (`refresh_days`, default 30, renewed on use), both stored SHA-256-hashed. A session ends `session_days` (default 90) after sign-in however often it is refreshed (401 `session_expired`); the remember-this-device token lasts `remember_device_days` (default 30). Rotation only happens for the token presented; presenting an already-rotated token revokes the session. Tokens go in the `Authorization` header; only `/api/ws` also accepts `?access_token=`. Signing out, `DELETE /account/sessions/{id}`, `POST /account/sessions/revoke-others` (every session except the caller's), a password or Secret Key change (every other session; the device making the change stays signed in), and disabling or deleting an account send `{"type":"logout"}` to exactly those sessions' sockets and close them; a 30 s check closes sockets whose session ended any other way. `POST /account/devices/{device_id}/forget` signs out every session of that device and forgets it, including its remember token (its next sign-in asks for two-step login again and sends the new-device email); the sweeper clears expired remember tokens and forgets devices with no session that haven't signed in or refreshed for 90 days; refresh updates `last_seen` at most hourly. Web vault and desktop: Settings → Sessions has "Forget…" on each session row (servers with `forget_device`), with a confirmation counting that device's sessions; `current: true` signs this app out and drops its remember token (`forgetDevice` in `vault.ts`). Each session records the app that signed in (`X-PassAuth-Client: <name>/<version>`: web, desktop, extension, ios, android, macos-autofill; checked as `<name>/<version>` of at most 32 `[A-Za-z0-9-_.+]` each, updated on refresh), listed as `client`.
- 2FA: TOTP, email OTP, security keys / passkeys (WebAuthn), recovery codes. Wrong codes and failed security-key assertions are counted per account (`two_step_attempts_per_hour`, default 10, then a 1-hour pause and an email alert whose button opens Settings → Sessions), not only per sign-in attempt. New-device login sends an email alert. Turning on an authenticator app or email codes (`/account/2fa/{totp,email}/enable`) needs `x-reauth-token` (403 `reauth_required` without it), checked first and used up only on success, so a mistyped code doesn't need another master-password prompt; the apps ask for the master password in the same form. Every two-step change (a method on or off, new recovery codes, a security key added or removed) is emailed ("Two-step login changed on your PassAuth account"). Live-sync sockets are re-checked against `require_two_step` at every connection check: a failing account gets `{"type":"two_step_required"}` and close 1008 `two_step_required`, and the apps stop live sync and show the two-step gate.
- Security keys (`crates/server/src/passkeys.rs`, `webauthn-rs` "security key" ceremonies: user verification preferred, no resident key, no attestation check, ES256/ES384/RS256): the relying party is `DOMAIN` (RP ID = its host, allowed origin = the URL; behind a proxy it must be the public https address, else `GET /account` reports `passkeys_available: false` and the endpoints answer 400). Credentials are stored as webauthn-rs `SecurityKey` JSON in `passkey_credentials` (migration v4; counter and backup flags updated after each use, `last_used_at`), up to 10 per account; a `two_factor` row `provider = passkey` marks the method on while at least one exists, so login challenges list `"passkey"` among `providers`. Challenge state is kept in memory for 5 minutes like email codes: registrations in `App.passkey_registrations` keyed by a single-use `registration_id` (`POST /account/2fa/passkey/register/start` needs `x-reauth-token`, returns creation options with the user's other keys excluded; `…/register/finish {registration_id, name, credential}` stores the key and, as the first method, returns the recovery codes once; `DELETE /account/2fa/passkey/{id}` needs reauth), sign-ins inside the pending two-factor token (`POST /auth/2fa/passkey/start {two_factor_token}` → request options with `allowCredentials`; `POST /auth/2fa/passkey/finish {two_factor_token, credential, remember?}` → the same session and remember-device handling as `/auth/2fa/verify`). Only the web vault in a browser registers them (`web/src/lib/webauthn.ts`). To use one for two-step login, apps that can't run WebAuthn for the server's relying party (desktop WebView, extension, iOS, Android) use the **key page** hand-off (feature `two_step_key_page`, listed only for an https `DOMAIN`; with http the method stays web-vault-only): `/auth/2fa/passkey/start` also returns `key_page` (`DOMAIN` + `/two-step/key`); the app opens it only when it is https and its host equals both the options' `rpId` and the host of the server it is signing in to, as `/two-step/key#v=1&options=<base64url JSON as the server returned it>&return=app|page&state=<16 random bytes, base64url>` in a browser (desktop: system browser; extension: a centred popup window; iOS: `ASWebAuthenticationSession`; Android: Auth Tab / Custom Tab). The page (`web/src/lib/key-page.ts`, `pages/TwoStepKey.tsx`; public, skips the WASM and boot, never calls the API, stores nothing, shows nothing about the account, waits for a button press) passes `navigator.credentials.get` only an allowlisted request (challenge, rpId = its own host, a non-empty allowCredentials of public-key ids with optional transports, timeout, userVerification; extensions and everything else dropped). Before offering the key it asks `GET /api/auth/2fa/passkey/context?challenge=` (public, 30 a minute per address; `{client, device_name, ip, started_at}` while that challenge belongs to a two-step sign-in still waiting for its key, else 404; never the email, user id or token; `/start` remembers the calling app from `X-PassAuth-Client`, the visitor address and the time) and shows the sign-in's device or app, address and age with "If you didn't just start signing in to PassAuth, press Cancel and change your master password."; an ended or unknown sign-in gets no button. It sends the result only to a fixed target, echoing `state`: `passauth://two-step-key#v=1&state=…&credential=…` (apps) or `<origin>/two-step/key/done#…` (the extension reads it from its own window's tab via `tabs.onUpdated`); Cancel sends `error=cancelled`. The app accepts one answer while it waits, only with its own `state` (credential or exactly `error=cancelled`) and only when the signed `clientDataJSON` challenge equals the challenge it opened (iOS: `ASWebAuthenticationSession` closes on any `passauth://` answer, so a wrong one shows an error and the next try starts over), and finishes `/auth/2fa/passkey/finish` with the two-factor token it never let out of the app. Apps waiting for the browser show "PassAuth never asks for your master password or Secret Key in the browser." The extension keeps the token with the pending sign-in (a finish with another token is refused; a new sign-in closes an open key window) and leaves only the key window's top-level frame on the key page's origin to the browser's own authenticators. Known limit: someone who already has the master password (and Secret Key) and runs an app that registers `passauth://` can start a sign-in and send the user the real key page; the context line and warning are the defence, as with approving a sign-in from another device. The key page is server-served code like the web vault, so a compromised server could change it; no page markup is trusted by the apps. iOS/macOS AutoFill and Android's autofill/credential services keep hiding the method (sign in in the app). Older servers: the apps keep the old "use a recovery code" text. A counter rollback on assertion (`CredentialPossibleCompromise`: the key may have been cloned) is logged at warn level with user and credential id and emails the owner, besides counting as a wrong code; `/auth/2fa/passkey/start` is limited to 20 challenges per account per 5 minutes. Any future admin "reset two-step login" must clear `passkey_credentials` together with the `two_factor` rows.
- Rate limits key on the TCP peer, or on `IP_HEADER` when the peer is a trusted proxy (`TRUSTED_PROXIES`, default loopback and private networks), using the right-most untrusted hop; IPv6 is bucketed by /64. Sign-in allows `signin_attempts_per_minute` per address (default 30; prelogin twice that). The limiter keeps three stores (`state.rs`), so flooding one never resets another: address keys are capped at 100k (when full, ended windows go first, then the oldest tenth); per-target keys (signed-in user, two-step sign-in in progress, emergency grant, the server-wide sign-in-request cap) are made only after the target is validated and are never dropped for room; per-email ceilings (sign-ins 100 per 5 minutes, sign-in requests shown 5 per 15 minutes) are fixed slot tables (2^18 and 2^14 counters) indexed by SHA-256 of the email under a per-process random key, so every email is counted alike (no account-existence signal), nothing is evicted, and a shared slot only makes the limit stricter. A Send's wrong passwords are counted in its row (`sends.wrong_passwords`/`wrong_since`, migration 12; 100 per hour with one conditional UPDATE, survives restarts, reset when the owner sets a new password; while locked the right password also gets 429). Failed sign-ins are recorded by one background task through a bounded queue (1024) after the answer is sent, so the failure path does no database work and answers alike for known and unknown emails (`resend_verification` still writes only for existing accounts, and registration's 409 `account_exists` reveals accounts by design).

### Sharing (organizations)
- Vault keys are sealed to members' X25519 public keys. Sealed boxes are anonymous on their own; since migration 14 whoever seals a vault key signs the share and the vault's signed member roster (see **Signed sharing** below, which closes security review F2/F3 for apps that check it). Clients still never pick a shared (`key_kind = sealed`) vault as the default save target, only the user's own `account` vault. Remaining limits: in a vault without a manifest (see **Vault manifests**) a server can withhold `deleted` entries or recent revisions of items from a device that never saw them (a device that did flags an older copy by its edit counter `rev`); shares from before ("not yet signed") stay as trustworthy as before until an owner or admin re-signs the vault; and moving items between vaults is not possible (the AAD binds item and vault id).
- Ownership comes only from creating a vault: `add_member` refuses `owner`, and nobody can change or remove a member at or above their own role. Membership changes, item writes and key rotation lock the vault row, and rotation re-checks members and items inside its transaction (each item exactly once).
- Public keys are shown as fingerprints for out-of-band verification. The owner-side check (before sealing a vault key to a new member) protects the vault key: a server that substitutes the member's key fails the comparison. For a share from before (no signature) the invitee-side fingerprint is not a proof, so the apps label it "Fingerprint your server lists for <email>" with "Accept only invitations you expected."; a signed share is, once the signer's identity fingerprint is compared (Signed sharing). Settings → Account shows the user's own fingerprint computed from the unlocked key, never the server's `fingerprint` field.
- Only clients can replace a vault key (the server never has it). Removing a member is one request, `POST /vaults/{id}/members/{user}/remove`, that carries the new key for every remaining member and every item re-encrypted under it, applied in one transaction with the removal (same checks as `/rotate`), so nobody is removed without the key change. When someone leaves on their own, is removed with the older `DELETE /vaults/{id}/members/{user}`, or deletes their account, the server sets `vaults.rotation_pending` (migration v5); `/sync` reports it and the web vault / desktop app shows owners and admins "Finish securing this vault" until one of them calls `/rotate`, which clears it.
- **Invitations** (migration v8, feature `vault_invitations`): sharing a vault with someone who isn't a member yet (`POST /vaults/{id}/members`, unchanged body; answer adds `status`) makes an `invited` membership holding the sealed key (`vault_members.status`, `invited_by`, `invited_at`; rows from before are `active`); a role or key change for an existing member or invitee keeps the status. Until the invitee accepts, nothing of the vault reaches them: `/sync` (full and delta: `vaults`, items, cursor), `/sync/revision`, live-sync vault numbers and every read/write check count only `active` memberships, so apps from before never show an unaccepted vault and nobody can put a vault, or logins planted in it, into someone's apps without consent. Every `/sync` lists `invitations` [{vault_id, role, wrapped_key, meta, invited_by {id, email, name, public_key} | null, invited_at}]; apps open the sealed key to show the name and the sharer's fingerprint. `POST /vaults/{id}/invitation/accept` (repeatable) makes it a membership and the vault arrives whole at the next sync; `DELETE /vaults/{id}/invitation` declines; owners/admins cancel with `DELETE /vaults/{id}/members/{user}` (or `/remove` with a new key). Decline, cancel and an invitee's account deletion set `rotation_pending` like a member leaving: the invitation handed out the vault key. Invitees count for key replacement (`/rotate` and `/remove` need their key too) and get a new revision (live-sync message without vault numbers) when their invitation's key or name changes or the vault goes. With email on, the invitee gets "You've been invited to a shared vault" naming the sharer, linking to `/settings/vaults`. New invitations: 100 per hour per sharer. Web vault and desktop app: invitations are part of the delta snapshot (`delta.ts`; `invitations.ts` `readInvitations` drops malformed entries; each answer replaces the list); `vault.ts` opens each sealed key only to read the vault's name (`invitationName`, freed at once, cached) and keeps `State.invitations` while unlocked. Settings → Vaults & sharing (`/settings/vaults`) lists them first: vault name (or "can't be opened", with Decline only), sharer name and email, their key fingerprint with the out-of-band check, and the role, with Accept…/Decline… in a ConfirmDialog (Decline says the managers will be asked to replace the key). A dismissible notice above the item list, a count on the sidebar's Settings link and a badge on the Vaults & sharing tab show waiting invitations; they arrive and leave by live sync. Sharing: members with `status: invited` show "Invited" and "Cancel invitation" (the remove-with-new-key flow; the members list includes invitees, so every key replacement seals to them).
- Vault key epoch: `vaults.key_epoch` (migration v6) starts at 0 and goes up by 1 with every key replacement (`/rotate`, `/members/{user}/remove`, in the same transaction; both return `key_epoch`). `/sync` sends each vault's epoch. Writes carrying data encrypted with, or wrapping, a vault key send the epoch they used: `POST /items`, `PUT /items/{id}` (the target vault's when moving), each item of `/items/import`, `PUT /vaults/{id}`, `POST /vaults/{id}/members`, and rotate/remove bodies (the epoch being replaced). Any other epoch gets 409 `vault_key_changed` and nothing is written; with no epoch the write is accepted as before (older clients). Clients then sync, encrypt again and retry once: a create keeps its item id; an update retries only when the synced item's content and vault are unchanged, otherwise it stops with "changed on another device" so nobody's edit is overwritten unseen; rotate and remove sync and ask the user to check the members and try again. This stops a client that hasn't synced since a member was removed from saving items under the old key, which nobody could decrypt afterwards.
- Server writes (`crates/server/src/vault/`: `sync.rs`, `items.rs`, `sharing.rs`): every vault write goes through `VaultWrite`, one transaction that locks the vault rows in id order (no deadlocks when moving items between vaults), checks the caller's role (404 for non-members) and the key epoch, makes the change, then gives every member a new sync revision in the same transaction (PostgreSQL first locks the user rows in id order) and commits; members' sockets are told afterwards. Removals give out revisions before the membership goes. Revisions never go backwards. Each write also takes the vault's next `seq` in the statement that locks it, so a vault's writes are numbered in commit order, and stamps every item row it writes with it (moves: the target's number; rotation: every item). Items deleted for good (also by the trash sweeper) or moved out leave a tombstone in `item_tombstones (vault, item, seq, deleted_at)`; creating or moving an item into a vault removes its tombstone there. A move takes a number in both vaults. `commit` checks quotas first. A vault with a manifest (see Vault manifests) also gets its next manifest in that transaction (`VaultWrite::manifests`, called after the item statements; compare-and-swap on `manifest_seq`), and the lock statement returns the current `manifest_seq`. The same transaction writes the change's activity-log events and, for edits and trashing, the item's previous row as a version. Revisions go to the vaults' active members (with vault numbers) and to invitees whose invitation changed (without); PostgreSQL locks all those user rows in one statement in id order.


### Signed sharing (sender-authenticated vault keys, migration 14, feature `signed_sharing`)
Closes security review F2/F3: without it a malicious server could (a) seal a vault key of its own to a member (a forged invitation "from" a colleague, planted logins), (b) serve an older `wrapped_key`/`key_epoch` after a key replacement, (c) make a key replacement seal the new key to members it chose. Round 2 (2026-11-16, after a review of round 1: H1–H4, M1–M2, L1–L5) added the X25519 cross-signature, vault admission (consent, grandfathering, tombstones), key ids with retired keys, epoch-bound account wraps, member reviews and the roster hierarchy. Round 3 (2026-11-16, re-review of round 2: 1 High, 2 Medium, 6 Low) added the `pin` flag and `pinsObserve` (re-signing marks a vault signed), consent for unsigned key replacements, grandfathering only by the unlock that created the identity and only for unsigned rows, short retired key ids (1024 per vault), the highest trusted roster version and hash per vault, no digest from blocked reviews, safer tombstone merges and NFKC/IDNA email comparison. Round 4 (2026-11-16, final core review: 1 High, 2 Medium, 2 Low, 4 Info, plus the web/extension review's "puppet signer" High) made key pin merges monotone (retired key ids never shrink; re-joins are marked with `rejoined_at`; a tombstone absorbs the pins it beats), strips invisible characters from emails, binds review digests to the version and previous roster, added `pinsAccept` and the `own` mark, asks for consent for any new key on a vault without signed history, and bounds retired key ids (a core budget plus 30 key replacements per vault and hour on the server). Round 5 (2026-11-16, core and client reviews) pins a roster version only ever with its hash (`pinsSetEpoch` replaced by `pinsRecordRotation`, which requires it), asks for consent when a vault is signed for the first time at the same key (`first_signed`), binds grandfathered vaults to their key id, adds `pinsMergeLastKnown` (a device's last-known copy merged without overwriting newer person pins) and refuses merging pins of another own identity, carries consent flags into invitations (`invitation`, `held`, `own_vault`), adds `declinable` and `pinsDecline` (a decline never gives up a held vault), orders a later leave before an unseen re-join (`rejoined_over`), strips default-ignorable characters from emails (normalizing twice gives the same), refuses unknown observation fields, authenticates restored sessions (`Account.matchesBundle`), and counts key replacements on the server only once they passed every check. Core: `crates/core/src/sharing.rs`; server: `crates/server/src/signing.rs` (account side), `crates/server/src/vault/signed.rs` (vault side); tests `crates/server/src/tests/signing.rs`; byte-exact vectors `testdata/vectors.json` `signedSharing` (cross-checked independently of the core by `testdata/generate-vectors.mjs`: XChaCha20-Poly1305 opening, XEdDSA in BigInt arithmetic, HKDF/HMAC, Ed25519, rosters signed by the script itself). The server checks shapes, sizes and every signature against the signer's stored identity, and that each new roster follows the stored one (so garbage is never stored), but it can't vouch for anything: **every check below that says "the app" must be done by each client itself**, against identities it pinned.

**Canonical bytes** (everything signed or hashed): a sequence of fields, each `u32 big-endian length ‖ bytes`; the first field is the domain tag; text is UTF-8; numbers (`u64`) are an 8-byte big-endian field (so `00000008 ‖ n`); a list is its count (a number) followed by its elements' fields. Ids are 1–128 bytes without control characters. Never JSON. Signatures are Ed25519 (RFC 8032, deterministic), verified with `verify_strict` (no small-order keys, no malleable signatures); keys and signatures are base64url without padding. SHA-256 everywhere a hash is named.

**Signing identity** (one per account, Ed25519, cross-signed by the account's X25519 key):
- Seed: 32 random bytes; `wrapped_signing_key` = the seed encrypted with the Account Key (XChaCha20-Poly1305 as everywhere, associated data `passauth/signing-key/v1/<account id>`).
- Identity statement `["passauth/identity/v1", user id, X25519 public key (32), Ed25519 public key (32)]`, signed twice: by the Ed25519 key (`identity_signature`) and by the X25519 key as an **XEdDSA** signature (`x25519_signature`; Signal's "XEdDSA and VXEdDSA" rev. 1: the clamped X25519 secret as an Edwards scalar, negated if needed so the Edwards public key's sign bit is 0, 64 random bytes as `Z`; `xeddsa_sign`/`xeddsa_verify`/`xeddsa_public_key` in the core, built on curve25519-dalek). It verifies as Ed25519 (`verify_strict`) under the Edwards form of the X25519 key, `y = (u − 1)/(u + 1)` with sign bit 0; a non-canonical `u` (≥ p or top bit set), an off-curve or small-order key is refused. The cross-signature is what binds a signing key to the X25519 key: without it anyone could pair their own Ed25519 key with someone else's X25519 key (review H1). `signing_key`, `identity_signature` and `x25519_signature` come all three or not at all (legacy); there is no unsigned upgrade path.
- Identity hash: SHA-256 of `["passauth/identity-hash/v1", user id, X25519 key (32), Ed25519 key (32) or an empty field]`. A **legacy** identity (account without a signing key yet) hashes an empty Ed25519 field. **Identity fingerprint** = the first 15 bytes of the identity hash in base32, groups of four (`XXXX-XXXX-XXXX-XXXX-XXXX-XXXX`), over user id and both keys; the older X25519-only `fingerprint` (crypto.rs) stays for legacy flows and Settings of apps from before. Apps show the identity fingerprint wherever a fingerprint is compared once the account has a signing identity.
- **Membership vs authority**: a statement naming a recipient's or member's identity hash may hold their full or their legacy hash for *membership* (`VerifiedIdentity::matches`, `rosterMemberRole`: the same user and X25519 key, which the cross-signature binds to the signing key). A legacy listing grants **no signing authority** anywhere: a roster's owner/admin entries and share signers count only with exactly their full identity (`is_exactly`, `rosterSignerRole`), and the core refuses to sign a roster listing an owner or admin without a signing identity. A full identity served without its signing key is not a match (a downgrade).
- `PUT /account/identity {signing_key, identity_signature, x25519_signature, wrapped_signing_key}` (exact base64url lengths 43/86/86/98; 10 per hour per user): 400 `bad_signature` unless both signatures verify over the account's own id, its stored X25519 key and the signing key; 200 `{ok, created: true}` (event `account.identity_created`, the account's devices get a sync revision), the same record again 200 `{created: false}`, any other 409 `identity_exists`. **Set once**: there is no way to replace it (the apps have no X25519/identity key change flow; other users pinned it). A password or Secret Key change keeps it (it is wrapped by the Account Key, which those don't change).
- `/sync` `user.identity` = `{signing_key, identity_signature, x25519_signature, wrapped_signing_key}` or `null`; `/users/lookup`, member rows and share signer snapshots add `signing_key`, `identity_signature`, `x25519_signature` (null for legacy); `invited_by` (display only, never verified: the share's `signer` is) carries them with its `id` (no duplicate `user_id` any more; the core reads `id` as `user_id`).
- **App, at every unlock** (when signed sharing is on, see step 0): if `user.identity` is null and the key pins don't remember an own identity → `Account.newSigningIdentity()`, `PUT /account/identity` with its `record` (on 409 `identity_exists` sync again and open the stored one); else `Account.openSigningIdentity(user.identity)` → keep the `SigningIdentity` handle while unlocked (free it on lock). Then `pinsCheckOwnIdentity(pins, user.identity ? identity.identityHash() : null)`: `new` → `pinsSetOwnIdentity` and write the key pins; `match` → fine; `mismatch` (the pins remember an identity and `/sync` lists none or another) → `identity_mismatch`. `identity_mismatch` or `decrypt_failed` from opening → **paused**: sign nothing, refuse every sealed (shared) row, keep checking the epochs of account-wrapped vaults; show "Your server lists a signing key that isn't yours. Sharing is paused on this device." Settings → Account shows `SigningIdentity.fingerprint()` (computed locally, never the server's).
- **Who "me" is**: device memory and every "is this me" check use the account id the core authenticated (the unlocked account's id = `legacyIdentity().user_id`), never `/sync` `user.id`; a sync whose `user.id` differs is refused. Device memory is keyed by server base URL + account id. **The id is always one the core authenticated** (round 5, iOS H): a master-password unlock (the bundle opened under the Account Key), or a restored session (Face ID / timeout "never" / extension or AutoFill session bytes) checked with `Account.matchesBundle(storedBundle)` (`account_matches_bundle` → UniFFI `Account.matches_bundle(bundle_json)`): restore with `accountFromSessionBytes(storedBundle.account_id, bytes)`, then refuse the session (ask for the master password) unless `matchesBundle` is true — the bundle's private-key blob opens under the Account Key only with associated data bound to its real account id, so a server can't swap in a bundle with another id. A `/sync` or background-refresh bundle is stored only when `matchesBundle` is true for the unlocked account (another `account_id` is never stored). Device memory owned by another user id under the same local account and server is unreadable (fail closed), never empty.

**Key pins** (trust on first use, plus the account's vault memory): sealed with the Account Key (associated data `passauth/key-pins/v1/<account id>`), JSON `{version, pins: [{user_id, email, public_key, signing_key|null, identity (hash), verified, first_seen (Unix s)}], epochs: {<vault id>: {key_epoch, key_id, retired: [retired key id…], signed_since: <roster version>|null, roster_version_max?: <highest trusted roster version>, roster_hash?: <its rosterHash>, rejoined_at?: <roster version a re-join accepted>, own?: true}}, own_identity: <identity hash>|null, grandfathered: [vault id…]|null, left: {<vault id>: {pin: <epoch pin>|null, roster_version, left_at}}}` (unknown fields of newer apps are kept; each user once; each pin's `identity` must be its keys' hash; at most 190 KiB of JSON, `too_large` beyond: refused, never trimmed). The keys of `epochs` are the vaults this account **accepted**. `own`: the account holds (or held) the vault with its own `account` wrap (set by `pinsObserve`/`pinsAccept` from an `account` row). `rejoined_at`: set when accepting cleared a tombstone. **Retired key id budget** (round 4 L5; every change the core makes compacts to it, so key replacements alone can't push the pins past 190 KiB): at most 1024 per vault, 4096 across all accepted vaults (beyond it the longest lists lose their oldest ids first, never below 64 per vault), 64 per tombstone (whose pin keeps only key, retired ids, roster versions and the highest roster's hash). The server also allows at most 30 key replacements (`/rotate`, `/members/{user}/remove`) per vault and hour (429 `rate_limited`), counted only once a replacement passed every check, inside its transaction before the commit (round 5 L: a member's refused requests can't use up the vault's budget; over the limit the replacement rolls back).
- Server: `users.key_pins` + `key_pins_seq`. `GET /account/key-pins` → `{key_pins: <sealed>|null, seq}`; `PUT /account/key-pins {key_pins, seq}` stores by compare-and-swap (`seq` = stored + 1, else 409 `key_pins_changed`; ≤ 256 KiB base64url; 120 per hour; bumps the account's sync revision). `/sync` `user.key_pins_seq` tells devices to `GET` again when it differs from their copy.
- App: `Account.sealKeyPins(pins, seq)` seals with `version = seq`; `Account.openKeyPins(sealed, seq)` refuses another version (`rollback`). Each device keeps the highest `seq` it opened and its last pins (device-local, per account; like `pa.roster.*`): a server `seq` below it is a rollback → keep using the device's copy and show "Your server sent an older list of known keys". On 409 `key_pins_changed`: `GET`, open, `mergePins(server's, this device's unsent changes)` (this device's newer, unsent decision wins for the same user; epochs (round 4 H1): retired key ids only grow — a key either side retired never wins, whatever its epoch, the other side's pin does and the retired key stays retired; otherwise the higher epoch, at the same epoch the server's key (a different key at the same epoch is a server fork and shows as `conflict` wherever the other key is served); the earlier `signed_since`, the higher `roster_version_max` (same version: the server's hash, so a fork shows as `roster_forked`); tombstones (round 4 M2, L4): two tombstones merge (higher roster version, merged pins, later `left_at`); a pin beats a tombstone only with a `rejoined_at` above the tombstone's roster version (an explicit re-join after it) **and** a `rejoined_over` (the `left_at` of the tombstone that re-join cleared) at or after the tombstone's `left_at` (round 5 L: a later leave from a device that never saw the re-join wins; device clocks order the two), otherwise the pin is folded into the tombstone (its keys merged into the tombstone's pin, the tombstone's version raised to the pin's highest roster), so a stale pin never undoes a leave and the tombstone forgets nothing; own identity: the server's, else ours — **both remembering different own identities → `identity_mismatch`** (round 5: a device the server gave a fake identity must not spread its grandfathered vaults or epoch pins; the app merges nothing, writes nothing and pauses sharing); grandfathered: the union of both maps (a vault listed with two keys keeps the server copy's), since only an unlock that created the identity writes a non-empty one), write `seq + 1` again (one retry, then report). **Unsent changes are kept until written** (round 5, Android H): every pins change (accepts, own key replacements, tombstones, signer pins) is persisted in device memory as unsent at once, merged on top of every copy loaded (`mergePins(loaded, unsent)`), and cleared only after a `PUT` succeeded; a failed `PUT` never leaves the change only in session memory, and the device never replaces its sealed copy with the server's while the merged result differs and is unwritten. **Whenever a newer server copy arrives** (a higher `seq` at a sync, not only on 409 or with unsent changes) and there are no unsent changes, the device merges its last-known pins into it with **`pinsMergeLastKnown(server's, device's last-known)`** (round 5 M1; UniFFI `pins_merge_last_known`): only what never goes back is taken from the device copy (epoch pins: retired ids, the higher key, `signed_since`, the highest roster with its hash, `own`; tombstones; grandfathered vaults; the own identity; person pins the server lacks; `verified`/`first_seen` for the **same** identity), while for a person pinned with **another** identity the server copy wins (another device decided later). It writes the result back only if it differs from the server's: a device's knowledge (retired keys, tombstones, rosters) is never dropped because another device wrote first, and two devices that differ only in person pins don't rewrite each other's pins in a loop (which would also use up `PUT /account/key-pins`' hourly budget). `mergePins` ("ours wins") is only for unsent changes. Both throw `identity_mismatch` as above. **Device trust memory writes merge, never overwrite** (higher pins `seq` with its sealed copy, the higher trusted roster per vault, the union of "seen signed" marks), under a cross-process lock where several processes share it (iOS app + AutoFill extension).
- `pinCheck(pins, identity, email)` (after `verifyIdentity`; `email` = the one the server lists with it) is read-only: `new` → first time; `match` → fine (show "Verified" when `verified`); `upgraded` (pinned legacy, now the same X25519 key with a cross-signed signing key: cryptographically the same person since round 2) → re-pin with the email already pinned and its `verified` flag kept (round 4), and show the new fingerprint once; `changed` → **stop** whatever needed it (sealing, accepting, trusting a signature or roster) and show "<email>'s keys changed. Compare their fingerprint with them before you continue." with "Trust the new key" (a deliberate action: `pinSet(…, verified=true only if the user compared, now)`); `email_conflict` (no pin for this user id, but its email, compared in the `normalizeEmail` form (invisible characters removed, NFKC, lower case, trimmed, domain in IDNA ASCII; see Display), is pinned for another user id: a server-made account posing as someone known) → **stop** like `changed` ("Your server lists a new account for <email>, whose keys you already know under another account."). "Mark as verified" after an out-of-band comparison = `pinSet(…, verified=true)` only if the identity hash shown is still the pinned one (else "keys changed; compare again"). **Pins are written only after every check of the row passed** (`pinSet` of a signer, `pinsAccept`, `pinsObserve`); an invitation's signer is pinned only when the user accepts.
- **Display**: for a known identity the app shows the **pinned** email first, in the `normalizeEmail(email)` form: invisible characters (Unicode Cf — zero-width spaces and joiners, bidi controls, U+FEFF, tags —, Cc, Zl, Zp and every Default_Ignorable_Code_Point — the Hangul fillers U+115F, U+1160, U+3164, U+FFA0, the combining grapheme joiner U+034F, variation selectors U+FE00–FE0F and U+E0100–E01EF, U+180B–180D, U+17B4/17B5 (round 5 M); the set `web/src/lib/text.ts` strips) are removed before and after NFKC, the domain loses trailing dots and appears in punycode (`xn--…`, UTS #46 as the URL Standard's domain to ASCII), then loses trailing dots again (IDNA maps U+3002 and U+FF61 to "."), so a look-alike domain can't pass for a known one; when there is no domain or IDNA refuses it, an ASCII address stays as it is and otherwise every non-ASCII character (and `\`) is written as `\u{hex}`, so it can't look like an ASCII address (round 4 M3). Normalizing twice gives the same (round 5: apps may store the normalized email and compare it again). The core's ICU data and the browsers' Unicode version may differ for characters assigned after the older one (a remaining limit). Local parts are compared as they are after this: no confusables skeleton (no maintained crate for it in the tree), so a look-alike local part is a *new* identity, shown with its fingerprint (a remaining limit); a different email the server lists now appears only as "listed by your server as …"; "Verified" never sits next to a server-supplied name or email. Every name or email shown is cleaned (one line, invisible and control characters stripped, at most 128 characters).

**Key ids, epochs and account wraps** (review H3): `key_commitment` = HMAC-SHA256 keyed with HKDF-SHA256(vault key, no salt, info `passauth/vault-key-commitment/v1`) of `["passauth/vault-key-commitment/v1", vault id, key epoch (u64)]` (an HKDF subkey since round 2, review I1); **key id** = HMAC-SHA256 keyed with HKDF-SHA256(vault key, no salt, info `passauth/vault-key-id/v1`) of `["passauth/vault-key-id/v1", vault id]`, independent of the epoch. `VaultKey.epochPin(vaultId, row.key_epoch)` → `{key_epoch, key_id}` (the *current* key); `VaultKey.keyId(vaultId)`.
- `epochCheck(pinned, current)`: no pin → `new`; a key id in the pin's `retired` list → `older` (whatever epoch it is served under); lower epoch → `older`; same epoch: same key id `same`, else `conflict`; higher epoch: a never-seen key id `newer`, the pinned key id `conflict` (an old key relabelled). `pinsEpochCheck(pins, vaultId, current, rosterVersion)` adds `downgraded`: the pin says the vault is signed since roster version N (`signed_since`), or was trusted up to roster version M (`roster_version_max`), and the roster this device trusts now is none or below that (so "seen signed" and "seen up to version M" follow the account to every device: a device with no memory of a vault can't be shown a shorter chain).
- Pinning a key: `pinsAccept` (and `pinsObserve` for `ok.pin`) pin a `new`/`newer` key: the previous key id goes to `retired` (newest last, at most 1024 per vault, the oldest drop), `signed_since` = the first trusted roster version (set once, never lowered or removed); at the same key only `signed_since` is set if still empty. Either way the highest trusted roster is raised to the observation's `roster_version` **with its `roster_hash`** — a pin's `roster_version_max` and `roster_hash` always come together, and `signed_since` only with a `roster_version_max` at or above it (round 5 H: a version pinned without its hash turned the `chain_hashes` check off, so a chain re-signed from version 1 by a server-made account passed; key pins breaking this are malformed). Older or conflicting keys are never pinned. On a tombstoned vault they throw `rollback` unless the re-share qualifies (below); clearing a tombstone sets the pin's `rejoined_at` to that roster version and `rejoined_over` to the tombstone's `left_at`. **Accepts (consents and invitations) use `pinsAccept(pins, observation)`** (round 4 I1): it runs `pinsAdmit` on the same observation and throws what it would refuse (`rollback` for left/older/conflict/downgraded/own_vault/held, `roster_forked`, `identity_mismatch` when paused), then pins and marks an `account` row `own`. **A key this device itself just gave the vault** (its own key replacement, step 5, or re-signing, step 6) is recorded with `pinsRecordRotation(pins, vaultId, keyKind, epochPin, rosterVersion, rosterHash)` (UniFFI `pins_record_rotation(pins_json, vault_id, key_kind, current_json, roster_version, roster_hash)`): `keyKind` = this account's own row (`account`, or `sealed` when it sealed the key to itself), `rosterVersion` **and** `rosterHash` (`rosterHash(signed roster)`) both or neither (one alone throws); it is `pinsAccept` of that row as a signed one, so it can't pin an older or conflicting key or a lower or forked roster. `pinsSetEpoch` / `pins_set_epoch` are removed (round 5).
- **Retired key ids** are stored as the first 12 bytes of the key id (base64url, 16 characters; review round 3 L4): a server can't make a key with a chosen id (it never holds vault keys) and only members' apps replace keys, so a collision needs ~2^96 work. Full 32-byte ids from before are still read and compared by the same prefix. **Apps never replace a key without the user's action**, also on `rotation_pending` (they show "Replace the key" and wait): every replacement retires an id, and replacements a server could trigger at will would push old ids out of the list.
- **Account wraps**: an `account` `wrapped_key` is the vault key encrypted with the Account Key. Two formats, told apart by the first byte (no trial decryption): **v1** (the cipher's `0x01` version byte first; associated data `passauth/vault-key/v1/<vault id>`, no epoch) and **v2** (`0x02` marker, then the ciphertext; associated data `passauth/vault-key/v2/<vault id>/<key epoch>`, bound to the epoch the vault has after the write). **Which one apps write is decided per sync since migration 16 (feature `account_wraps_v2`)**: `/sync` `user.account_wraps_v2` is true when every active session of the account (refresh or access token still valid) comes from an app known to read v2 (`X-PassAuth-Client` `web/` ≥ 0.12.0, `desktop/` ≥ 0.9.0, `macos-autofill/` ≥ 0.9.0, `extension/` ≥ 0.8.0, `ios/` ≥ 0.8.0, `android/` ≥ 0.8.0; missing, unknown or unparseable = no; server `auth::reads_bound_wraps`). It is a compatibility hint only: a server that lies can only make apps write v1, as before, and an absent field (older server) means false. With it true, apps write v2 for every new vault (`Account.newVaultKeyBound(vaultId, 0)`; the server starts every vault at epoch 0) and every own key replacement (`VaultKey.wrapForBound(account, vaultId, row.key_epoch + 1)`: the server's rotation gives the vault `key_epoch + 1`); else v1 (`newVaultKey`, `wrapFor`). Registration writes v1 (no sync yet). An app from before 0.8 that signs in later can't open vaults re-keyed in v2 meanwhile (accepted: those versions are no longer supported for that). Old-key replay is stopped by key ids and retired key ids in the account's key pins, which don't depend on the wrap format.
